## Future/Excluded

The following are explicitly outside the final launch product after product review. Do not re-add them as launch requirements without a direct product decision.

| Feature | Decision | Reason |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Marketplace / public community | Removed from product scope. | YOW is a private creative workspace, not a public community platform. |
| Collaboration / multi-user editing | Removed from product scope. | Shared editing, permissions, presence, conflict resolution, and collaboration support would create a different product. |
| Native mobile apps | Removed from product scope. | Responsive web usability remains the launch requirement. |
| Public project/profile sharing | Removed from product scope. | Launch promise should stay focused on private writing, planning, ownership, and export. |
| Read-only share links / player view | Future only; highest-value collaboration-adjacent addition if TTRPG demand justifies it. | A read-only player/GM handout view would address the biggest TTRPG competitive gap without full collaboration, but it still creates permissions, privacy, sharing, and support obligations that are outside final launch scope. |
| Custom/fantasy calendar engine | Future only unless TTRPG positioning changes. | YOW has eras, timelines, and history, but not an invented-calendar simulation engine. Kanka and World Anvil use this as a TTRPG differentiator; YOW should not imply calendar-engine support at launch. |
| Advanced AI agents | Removed from product scope. | Current AI architecture is intentionally bounded and user-directed; autonomous workflows are not necessary for launch. |
| EPUB export | Excluded unless publishing-output positioning changes. | Current DOCX/PDF/ZIP/export ownership path is more important. EPUB adds validation, formatting, reader-compatibility, and publishing-support expectations that are not worth launch scope unless YOW promises direct publication output. |
| Voice dictation | Excluded unless accessibility requirements change. | Browser dictation support is inconsistent and microphone/privacy expectations add QA burden. It is useful, but not necessary for the current product promise. |
| Publishing platform integrations | Removed from product scope. | Direct publishing integrations would add external API support, account linking, and platform-specific maintenance outside the private workspace model. |
| Native desktop app for Free/Monthly | Free and Monthly remain browser-only cloud plans. | Product decision 2026-07-04 moved the Lifetime tier to a downloadable desktop app (see PRD: Desktop Lifetime App and Local Vault). Desktop access is a Lifetime/Founder entitlement only; no desktop promise for Free or Monthly. |
| Extended template marketplace / community-submitted templates | Removed from product scope. | Built-in project-type templates are enough; user/community template distribution would recreate marketplace/community obligations. |
| Passage-level comments/annotations | Removed from launch scope. | Scene notes are sufficient for launch; true passage annotation would add editor complexity and data-loss risk without being central to YOW's private planning/export promise. |

## PRD: Map Builder Rebuild

### Product Summary

The Map Builder is final-launch scope. It should become a reliable worldbuilding and campaign-planning canvas, not a novelty drawing panel. It should let writers and GMs quickly create readable world, region, local, and interior maps; link places back to Locations; use grid overlays for movement/planning; export enough visual and metadata context to be useful; and feel stable under normal editing.

Map quality is required because D&D/TTRPG users will reasonably expect usable campaign-map support. The launch promise is planning-grade map creation and export, not live play, live player access, fog of war, initiative automation, or VTT replacement.

### Current Baseline

The current implementation already supports multiple maps per project, map types (world, region, local, interior), object layers, land/region/river/road/border paths, stamps, labels, linked locations, JSON import/export, basic style presets, snap, lock/hide, grouping, duplication, and layer ordering.

The biggest issues are interaction quality and output quality: zoom is too sensitive, selection and drag behavior are not intuitive, drawing modes are easy to confuse, closed water shapes are not treated as filled water, the Mountain line tool does not produce convincing mountains, and project exports currently preserve map metadata better than rendered visual plates.

### Goals

- Make the map canvas predictable: no accidental moves, wild zoom jumps, or unclear selection state.
- Make drawing tools understandable: users can tell whether they are selecting, panning, drawing, placing, or editing points.
- Support the core fantasy/cartography primitives writers expect: land, water, regions, routes, borders, labels, settlements, landmarks, terrain stamps, mountains, and interiors.
- Support optional grid overlays for movement and planning: square grid at minimum, clear sizing controls, opacity/color controls, snap-to-grid where useful, and map-scale labels that help a GM understand distance without turning YOW into a live tabletop.
- Preserve and export maps safely through project backup/restore, JSON import/export, and world bible exports.
- Keep map creation fast for non-artists: good defaults, stamp presets, natural variation, and enough styling without demanding design expertise.

### Non-Goals

- Professional GIS tooling, real geographic projection, or measured real-world cartography.
- Full image-editing software, raster painting, pressure-sensitive brush engines, or Photoshop-style layer effects.
- Real-time collaboration.
- Procedural world generation as the first rebuild milestone.
- Battle-map rules automation, fog of war, initiative tracking, or VTT replacement.

### Required Canvas Interaction

- Provide explicit Select, Pan, Draw, Place Stamp, Label, and Location modes with visible active-state labels.
- Wheel and trackpad zoom must be damped, bounded, and centered around the cursor; trackpad scroll should not launch the map from tiny to huge in one gesture.
- Add visible zoom controls: zoom in, zoom out, fit, reset to 100%, and current percentage.
- Add visible grid controls: show/hide grid, grid size, grid opacity, grid colour, snap-to-grid, and a plain movement scale label such as "1 square = 5 ft" or a custom unit.
- Space/Alt temporary pan should work reliably; cursor state should reflect pan, grab, drawing, dragging, resizing, and rotating.
- Selection must use hover highlights, selected outlines, clear handles, and predictable click behavior.
- Clicking empty canvas deselects unless a draw/place mode is active.
- Dragging an object should require a small movement threshold so normal clicks do not accidentally move it.
- Multi-select should support Shift/Cmd/Ctrl click, select all, clear selection, and layer-panel selection.
- Resize, rotate, and point-edit handles must be large enough at every zoom level and must not conflict with object selection.
- Undo/redo is required for create, move, resize, rotate, delete, duplicate, group, ungroup, style edits, point edits, map type changes, imports, and layer-order changes.
- Keyboard shortcuts should cover Delete, Escape, Enter-to-finish-path, Cmd/Ctrl+Z, Cmd/Ctrl+Shift+Z, Cmd/Ctrl+A, Cmd/Ctrl+D, plus optional one-key mode shortcuts after focus safety is confirmed.

### Required Map Tools

- Land: polygon, rectangle, and ellipse/circle landmasses with organic shoreline option, solid fill/stroke, point editing, and base-layer behavior.
- Water: filled lake/pond/sea polygons with clean solid water fill, editable edge, fill, stroke, organic edge, and optional wave texture. River remains a separate open-path tool for rivers/coastlines and visually blends intersecting river strokes like a paint brush.
- Regions: political or terrain areas with solid fills by default, borders, labels, and editable polygon points; terrain can still be deliberately set to a transparent background.
- Roads/Paths: open lines with solid/dashed styles, thickness, color, and optional route labels.
- Borders/Walls: open or closed path support with dashed/solid styles and clear visual distinction from roads.
- Stamps: rename "Forests and Stamps" to "Stamps"; forests become a Terrain stamp category/preset, not a separate tool label.
- Stamp placement: click-to-place, drag-to-place, recent/favorite stamps, search, category filters, rotation, scale, opacity, duplicate, and random variation controls.
- Mountains: either replace the current Mountain tool with convincing mountain symbols/ranges (natural spacing, size jitter, rotation jitter, ridge direction, density, and group editing) or remove it and rely on mountain stamps until quality is good.
- Labels: readable text labels with font size, color, optional curved labels for regions/routes, and collision-aware defaults where feasible.
- Locations: place new or existing Locations on the map; linked markers should update display names when the Location name changes and should navigate back to the Location detail.
- Interiors: interior maps are spatial-layout tools, not world/region maps indoors. They need distinct room/corridor/wall semantics, doors, furniture/object stamps, snap-to-grid defaults, and neutral terminology for prose/comic projects. D&D/TTRPG projects additionally use dungeon terminology, traps, secret doors, 5 ft movement language, and a grid-first dungeon-planning presentation.
- Grid overlays: square grid is required for launch; hex grid is optional only if it can be implemented cleanly without destabilising the map builder. Grid overlays must export visually and persist in map data.

### Data Model And Persistence

- Keep a versioned map schema with forward-compatible object records.
- Each object needs stable `id`, `type`, position, size, rotation, z/layer order, visibility, lock state, style metadata, points/faces where relevant, and linked entity IDs where relevant.
- Each map needs grid settings: enabled, grid type, size, opacity, colour, snap setting, and movement scale/unit metadata.
- Add explicit water-mass metadata rather than overloading river lines once a path is closed.
- Add mountain-range metadata only if the mountain tool is improved; otherwise remove the line-tool mode from available map types.
- Persist maps synchronously enough that refresh/navigation does not lose recent edits.
- Project ZIP export/restore must include all maps, object data, layers, linked location IDs, map type/style metadata, and any generated thumbnails.
- JSON map import/export must preserve unknown future fields and report invalid imports clearly.
- Deleting a linked Location should not crash the map; markers should become unlinked but keep their text unless the user chooses to remove them.

### Visual Output

- Canvas rendering should look intentionally cartographic at normal zoom: cleaner water, land, region, route, border, mountain/stamp, and label styling.
- Every map should maintain or generate a thumbnail/preview image for dashboard/export contexts.
- Grid overlays should be legible but not overpower map content, and should appear in exported visual plates when enabled.
- Project DOCX/PDF/world-bible exports should include map thumbnails or rendered plates where possible, not only metadata.
- Exports should list map labels, linked locations, regions, and notable stamps so a text-only export still contains useful context.

### UX Layout

- Rework the layout into three dependable zones: left tool palette, central canvas, right properties/layers inspector.
- Keep map selection, map type, style, export/import, undo/redo, and zoom controls in a stable top bar.
- The left palette should show only tools relevant to the current map type, with plain labels and tooltips.
- The Stamps panel should support search, category tabs/filter, favorites, recent stamps, and larger previews.
- The right inspector should separate Object Properties, Layers, and Map Settings with compact tabs or sections.
- Avoid hiding essential controls in advanced accordions when they are part of normal editing.
- Mobile/tablet can be view/edit-lite, but must not show a broken canvas. If full editing is desktop-first, the UI should say so clearly and provide safe viewing/export.

### QA Acceptance

- Create a world map with land, closed sea/lake, open river, regions, roads, borders, labels, settlements, stamps, and linked locations; refresh and verify everything persists.
- Create a region map with roads, towns, rivers, terrain stamps, and labels; verify selection, moving, resizing, rotating, point editing, grouping, and layer ordering.
- Create an interior map with rooms, walls, doors, windows, furniture, labels, and object stamps; verify map-type-specific tool filtering.
- Enable a square grid, set movement scale, change size/opacity/colour, use snap-to-grid, refresh, export, restore, and confirm the grid and movement metadata persist and render in visual exports.
- Verify wheel and trackpad zoom are controlled at desktop and laptop settings; zoom never jumps beyond min/max unexpectedly.
- Verify undo/redo for every common edit path.
- Verify invalid JSON import shows an error and valid JSON import preserves object data.
- Export a project ZIP, restore it, and confirm maps, layers, thumbnails, linked markers, and metadata survive.
- Export DOCX/PDF/world bible and confirm maps appear as useful visual or text plates.
- Test at 1280px desktop, 768px tablet, and 375px mobile; full editing may be desktop-first, but the UI must not overlap or trap controls.

### Implementation Phases

| Phase | Scope | Acceptance Criteria |
| --- | --- | --- |
| 1. Interaction stabilization | Zoom damping/bounds, explicit modes, hover/selection state, drag threshold, undo/redo, handle clarity, keyboard safety. | Partial implementation: zoom damping/bounds, undo/redo, drag threshold, Space/Alt temporary pan, hover highlights, clearer handles, cursor cues, and active-brush click-to-deselect landed. QA is deferred in `docs/QA_PLAN.md`. Still needs broader keyboard polish and deeper browser verification later. |
| 2. Tool semantics | Closed water masses, renamed Stamps tool, stamp workflow improvements, mountain improve/remove decision, clearer land/region/road/border behavior. | Partial implementation: closed water masses and Stamps label landed; weak Mountain line creation removed from active toolsets and quick-add controls so users rely on the Mountains stamp while legacy mountain-line objects still render. Still needs stamp workflow polish and clearer land/region/road/border behavior. |
| 3. Inspector and layers | Rework properties/layers/map settings, improve multi-select, grouping, lock/hide, z-order, and linked-location editing. | Partial implementation: right inspector now uses Object/Layers/Map tabs; map type is chosen in New Map forms and displayed read-only after creation; object properties include object-layer assignment and clearer Order naming; Layers supports layer creation, rename-on-blur, active-layer placement, visibility, lock, delete with object fallback, object stack selection, and lock-aware edit disabling. Still needs deeper linked-location navigation/edit polish and browser verification later. |
| 4. Visual quality | Better cartographic styling, reference-aligned fantasy stamp visuals, thumbnails/previews, label readability, map-type-specific polish. | Partial implementation: the stamp library now includes the 16 reference-image fantasy icons as extracted transparent PNG assets, with matching sidebar previews and placed stamp rendering. Still needs visual QA and later thumbnail/preview work. |
| 5. Export and restore | ZIP restore coverage, JSON forward compatibility, DOCX/PDF/world-bible map plates. | Partial implementation: project backup already preserves map records; HTML/world-bible and PDF map sections now render visual map plates instead of object inventory lists. Generated object-map preview plates render into HTML/PDF exports when no saved map image exists. Still needs deeper ZIP restore/browser verification later. |
| 6. Responsive safety | Desktop editing pass plus tablet/mobile view/edit-lite behavior. | The map builder is either usable or clearly constrained on each supported viewport, with no broken layout. |
| 7. Grid and movement planning | Optional grid overlay, movement scale, snap-to-grid, persisted grid settings, and visual/export support. | Required for final launch because tabletop GMs need movement/planning guidance. Square grid is required; hex grid is optional if low-risk. |

Hardened 2026-07-13: field length caps (title 200, message 8000, category 100, email 254, name 120 — over-length returns 400), per-IP sliding-window rate limit (5/hour → 429; in-memory, best-effort per warm serverless instance by design — no storage dependency added), and nodemailer upgraded to ^9.0.3 (`npm audit` reports 0 vulnerabilities). Validation and rate-limit helpers unit-tested in `tests/api/submit-feedback.test.js`. CORS restriction judged unnecessary: the route sets no permissive CORS headers, so cross-origin reads are already browser-blocked. | Hardened 2026-07-13 | Live-check one real feedback submission after the next deploy (tracked in QA plan Priority 11). |

Because `AuthContext.updateProfile()` writes `user_metadata` via `supabase.auth.updateUser()` — a call any signed-in user can also make directly, bypassing the app entirely — this let a Free user self-grant paid/Lifetime/Founder/beta status and desktop download/device access. `was_monthly` (gates the free-tier project lock) and `trial_started_at` (gates trial length) had the same shape of problem. Fixed 2026-09-01: entitlement now reads only from server-controlled `app_metadata` (`subscription_status`, `subscription_plan`, `beta_tester`, `was_monthly`); `trial_started_at` was dead code (nothing ever wrote it) and was removed in favor of the already-trustworthy `created_at`. `AuthContext.updateProfile()` now allowlists profile writes to genuinely harmless fields only (`PROFILE_METADATA_ALLOWLIST` in `membership.js`) as defense in depth — the actual security boundary is `getMembership()`/the API routes no longer reading `user_metadata` at all, since Supabase's client SDK lets a user write `user_metadata` regardless of what any app-layer wrapper allows. `api/stripe-webhook.js`'s `was_monthly` write moved from `user_metadata` to `app_metadata` (merged into the existing single write to avoid a stale-object overwrite bug). `api/register-paid-interest.js` stopped writing a redundant copy of `beta_tester`/`subscription_*` into `user_metadata` (app_metadata was already, and remains, the authoritative write). New `scripts/migrate_sanitize_user_metadata.mjs` (`--dry-run` / `--apply`) strips the now-untrusted entitlement keys from every existing account's `user_metadata`, with a manual-review report for any account where `user_metadata` claims entitlement `app_metadata` doesn't also grant. 16 new/updated tests (`membership.test.js`, `AuthContext.test.jsx`) cover the negative matrix. The CSP's `script-src` previously would have needed `'unsafe-inline'` for two inline `<script>` blocks in `index.html` (the pre-first-paint theme setter, and the Google Analytics config) — instead extracted both to `public/theme-init.js` and `public/ga-init.js` (byte-identical logic, referenced via `<script src="...">`), so `script-src` can require `'self'` with no inline exception, a real reduction in XSS blast radius rather than a CSP that only looks strict. Verified both the web build (`npm run build`) and desktop build (`npm run build:desktop`) produce correct output: both external files present in `dist/`, and `vite.config.mjs`'s existing `ga:start`/`ga:end` stripping still removes the whole GA block (now including the externalized `<script src="/ga-init.js">` tag) from the desktop build while keeping `/theme-init.js` on both platforms, exactly matching pre-change behavior. Full unit suite (577/577) and lint (0 errors) unaffected — `vercel.json` headers aren't exercised by local dev/test, hence the live-verification requirement above. CORS being separately broad on several API routes (audit item 21) is a different, larger fix (per-endpoint origin allowlisting) intentionally left out of this change. | Engineering |
| Stripe fulfillment was not idempotent (audit P0-05) | `api/stripe-webhook.js` had no processed-event ledger. Two concrete bugs followed: (1) `checkout.session.completed` and the corresponding first `invoice.paid` for a new maintenance/hosting-renewal subscription both called `extendMaintenance()`, adding two years of hosting for one payment (and any Stripe retry of either event could add more, since `extendMaintenance` is additive from the current expiry); (2) Founder allocation was only checked before Checkout creation, never atomically reserved at fulfillment, so two near-simultaneous Founder purchases near slot 100 could both succeed and exceed the advertised cap. Fixed 2026-09-01: new `stripe_processed_events` table (migration `20260901_stripe_webhook_idempotency.sql`) — the webhook inserts `event.id` before any fulfillment work; a unique-constraint violation means the event was already claimed (an earlier delivery or a concurrent retry) and processing is skipped; a handler error deletes the claim so a genuine later retry can still be processed. `checkout.session.completed` no longer extends maintenance itself — only the subscription's `invoice.paid` does, since Stripe subscriptions always generate an invoice for their first period as well as every renewal, making it the one canonical fulfillment point. Founder allocation now goes through a new `claim_founder_slot(uuid)` Postgres function (same migration) that uses `pg_advisory_xact_lock` to serialize concurrent callers and does an atomic check-then-reserve within one transaction; if the atomic claim loses the race after the customer already paid, the account is granted Lifetime instead (never left with nothing) and flagged via a new `user_profiles.founder_overflow_at` column for the owner to manually resolve the price difference or comp a slot. A full refund (`charge.refunded` with `refunded: true`) of a Founder charge calls the new `release_founder_slot(uuid)` function to free the slot counter — partial refunds are ignored. One-time purchases (`session.mode === 'payment'`) now check `payment_status === 'paid'` before fulfilling, and `checkout.session.async_payment_succeeded`/`async_payment_failed` are handled for delayed payment methods (e.g. bank debits) where `checkout.session.completed` can fire before payment actually clears. Raw exception messages no longer reach the client on a 500 (full detail is still logged server-side) — this was also flagged in the audit's P0-05 section specifically. `updateSubscriptionMembership()` (recurring plans) and `activateLifetimePlan()` (Lifetime/one-time, aside from Founder allocation) were confirmed already naturally idempotent — every field either overwrites with Stripe's current live state or short-circuits on an already-set value — so the ledger is defense-in-depth for those paths rather than fixing an active bug in them specifically. 22 tests in `tests/api/stripe-webhook.test.js` (event-dedup, the maintenance double-extension fix end-to-end, Founder claim success/overflow, refund release, payment-state gating, no-raw-error-leakage). New read-only `scripts/reconcile_stripe_fulfillment.mjs` flags Founder overcount, implausible `maintenance_expires_at` values (the pre-fix bug's fingerprint), and live Stripe-vs-Supabase subscription drift — the audit's "reconciliation tooling" requirement. Full detail: `docs/YOW_CODE_AUDIT_2026-09-01.md` P0-05 (lands with PR #38). | Code fixed 2026-09-01, PR pending review | Merged, and the user confirmed 2026-09-02 that `supabase/migrations/20260901_stripe_webhook_idempotency.sql` has been applied to production. Before real checkout is re-enabled: replay real Stripe test-mode events 2-3 times via the Stripe CLI against a deployed instance and confirm single fulfillment; run `scripts/reconcile_stripe_fulfillment.mjs` against production to check for accounts already affected by the pre-fix double-extension bug (fix the code path, not the historical drift it already caused — those existing over-extended accounts need a manual, explicit product decision, not an automatic correction); verify Founder concurrency directly by attempting two near-simultaneous test-mode Founder checkouts once slots are near the cap. This is the "Stripe fulfillment idempotency" item in `docs/QA_PLAN.md` Priority 0. | Engineering/QA |
| Storage acknowledged writes before durability (audit P0-07) | The IndexedDB and desktop-vault backends (`src/storage/indexedDbBackend.js`, `src/storage/desktopVaultBackend.js`) update an in-memory mirror synchronously (so the UI can echo an edit instantly) but persist to disk asynchronously off a queue — deliberate, but a failure in that queued persist previously only ever reached `console.error`. Nothing recorded it, no UI warned the user, and closing the tab/app right after could silently lose the edit even though the app had already shown it as accepted. Notably, `useStore.js` already had well-designed failure-visibility machinery (`markLocalWriteFailed`/`hasLocalWriteFailed`, a persistent dismissible banner in `App.jsx`) — but it was only ever fed from a synchronous `try/catch` that only the legacy/fallback `localStorage` backend actually throws through; the real production backends (IndexedDB, desktop vault) never triggered it. Same root cause independently defeated a second existing safeguard: `sceneContentStore.js`'s careful "only strip a scene's prose from `nf_scenes` metadata once its own content key is confirmed written" ordering, whose own `try/catch` had the identical blind spot. Fixed 2026-09-02: extracted the failure-tracking (and a new corrupt-JSON-read tracker) into a shared `src/storage/writeDurability.js` — a storage-layer module, not a store concern, so it can be fed from both `useStore.js`'s existing synchronous path and the backends' real async failure path (wired in `browserVaultAdapter.js`/`tauriVaultAdapter.js`). Both backends now retry a failing write 3× with exponential backoff before giving up (most real failures are transient — a blocked version-change upgrade, a momentary quota spike from another tab), expose `getDurabilityState()` (pending count + last error), and thread the failing *key* through to `onWriteError` so the existing warning banner and a new `beforeunload` guard (blocks navigation with a confirmation prompt — the one thing a browser actually lets you do about an in-flight async write it won't guarantee finishes) both work correctly. `projectStorage.js`'s `loadValue()` now distinguishes "key never had a value" from "value present but failed to parse" (genuine corruption) and records the latter — previously indistinguishable, both silently resolving to the default/empty value. The Manuscript editor's "Saved" indicator (`Manuscript.jsx`) previously reflected *only* cloud sync status — a Free/local-only session with no cloud sync at all always read "Saved" the moment the debounce timer cleared, with zero connection to whether the on-device write actually succeeded; it now shows a distinct red "Save failed — retrying" state (new `SaveIndicator` branch, `ManuscriptToolbar.jsx`) whenever a local write has failed, which always wins over a successful cloud sync (the point of "Saved" is that *this device* won't lose the edit). Full finding: `docs/YOW_CODE_AUDIT_2026-09-01.md` P0-07. | Fixed 2026-09-02, verified live in a real browser session (not just unit tests) | This closes the core mechanism — durability is now visible and automatically retried — but two of the audit's required-outcome items are deliberately **not** attempted here, and are logged as open follow-ups rather than silently left out: (1) **"keep a recoverable journal"** — a genuinely separate, larger piece of work (a secondary write-ahead log reconciled on next boot) beyond what this change does; today's recoverability is limited to "the in-memory mirror still has the latest value until the tab closes, and the next edit to a failed key naturally retries and clears the warning." (2) The `sceneContentStore.js` ordering safeguard's worst case is now *visible* (the warning banner/beforeunload guard fire) but not mathematically eliminated — `knownContentKeyIds.add(scene.id)` can still be marked optimistically before an async content-key write is confirmed, same as before; retries make actual persistent failure much rarer, but a determined adversarial timing (retries exhausted, user ignores the warning and closes anyway) could still exhibit the pre-fix narrow race. Needs QA: inject a real IndexedDB quota-exceeded error (not just a mocked throw) and confirm the banner/beforeunload guard/manuscript indicator all fire together on a real browser, then confirm they all clear once space is freed and the next edit succeeds. | Engineering/QA |
| CORS reflected arbitrary origins on 7 of 8 API routes (audit item 21) | `api/ai-settings.js`, `api/create-checkout-session.js`, `api/create-customer-portal.js`, `api/desktop-devices.js`, `api/get-download-links.js`, `api/get-founder-slots.js`, and `api/register-paid-interest.js` all set `Access-Control-Allow-Origin` to `req.headers.origin \|\| SITE_URL \|\| '*'` — verbatim reflection of whatever `Origin` header the caller sent, not an allowlist check. Only `api/ai-proxy.js` had the real fix (a proper `allowedOrigins()` allowlist, from the P0-02 remediation) — this left every other route open to a third-party site driving a cross-origin `fetch()` against them (with a bearer token the attacker's page already has by some other means) and reading the response, since the reflecting server told the browser it was fine to hand it back. Explicitly called out as intentionally deferred in the row above ("CORS being separately broad on several API routes (audit item 21) is a different, larger fix... intentionally left out of this change"). Full finding: `docs/YOW_CODE_AUDIT_2026-09-01.md`, finding #21. | Fixed 2026-09-03 | Extracted `api/ai-proxy.js`'s existing allowlist (`allowedOrigins()`) plus a new shared `applyCors(req, res, { methods, headers })` helper into `api/_lib/cors.js` (underscore-prefixed so Vercel doesn't treat it as its own route), and pointed all 8 routes (the 7 above plus `ai-proxy.js` itself, deduplicating its own copy) at the shared helper — every relative import uses an explicit `.js` extension, since an extensionless one in this exact `api/` directory previously took `ai-proxy.js` down in production (see the 2026-09-02 `FUNCTION_INVOCATION_FAILED` Bugs-table row below). `allowedOrigins()`'s logic is unchanged from `ai-proxy.js`'s existing P0-02 version (localhost dev origins, the Tauri desktop webview origins, `SITE_URL`, and the auto-populated `VERCEL_URL` for preview deployments) — no route's legitimate caller (web app, desktop app, Vercel preview) is newly blocked; the only behavior change is that a *disallowed* origin no longer gets echoed back. `api/ai-proxy.js` re-exports `allowedOrigins` so its existing test (`tests/api/ai-proxy.test.js`) needed no changes. New `tests/api/cors.test.js`: unit coverage of `applyCors` (allowed origin echoed + `Vary: Origin` set, unrecognized origin gets no header, no-Origin request gets no header, `SITE_URL` respected, method/header overrides), plus a regression-guard test that greps every `api/*.js` file for the old reflection pattern so a future route can't reintroduce it silently. Verified via `scripts/check-api-esm-resolution.mjs` (all 12 routes still import cleanly under Node's real ESM loader — this is the exact check that would have caught the P0-02-era incident), full suite (611/611, was 605 + 6 new), lint (0 errors), and production build all clean. Reviewed via `code-reviewer` (general `code-review` pass at medium effort: no findings) and `security-reviewer` (no findings — this is a pure hardening change, removing exploit surface rather than adding any). | Engineering |

| 2026-09-02: Login did not persist between bare-domain and `www` tabs | Authentication appeared to be lost when a signed-in user opened YOW in another tab through the other production hostname. Live inspection confirmed both `https://yourownworld.co.uk/*` and `https://www.yourownworld.co.uk/*` returned the app with `200` responses instead of consolidating onto one origin. Supabase persists browser sessions in origin-scoped `localStorage`, so the two hostnames necessarily had separate session stores even though they served the same app. | Fixed in code; deployment verification pending | After deployment, request the bare-domain root, `/login`, `/dashboard`, a deep project path, and a URL with query parameters; each must return a permanent redirect to the same path/query on `https://www.yourownworld.co.uk`. Sign in on `www`, open a bare-domain link in a second tab, and confirm it redirects to `www` and opens already authenticated. Existing users whose only session was stored on the bare domain may need to sign in once on `www` after the redirect first ships. | Added a host-conditional permanent redirect in `vercel.json` from the bare production domain to the canonical `www` origin, preserving the requested path. This removes the split-origin auth state rather than copying session tokens between origins. Added a config regression test in `src/config/vercelConfig.test.js` and auth-provider coverage proving a same-origin `SIGNED_IN` broadcast updates an already-open tab. |
| 2026-09-02: Project-area navigation tiles appeared below the dashboard stats and momentum panels | The main routes into Outline, Characters, Atlas, Lore, History, Ideas, and other enabled project areas were visually deprioritized beneath summary information. | Fixed 2026-09-02; focused automated QA passed | No further action needed. | `ProjectDashboard` now renders the enabled Project areas navigation before the stat row at every breakpoint. A DOM-order regression test in `ProjectDashboard.test.jsx` protects the requested hierarchy. |
| 2026-09-02: `/api/ai-proxy` returned `FUNCTION_INVOCATION_FAILED` (a 500) on every single request — including GET/OPTIONS, before any of the route's own logic ever ran — from the moment the P0-02 AI-proxy authorization fix (PR #39) was deployed to production | **Complete outage of the AI proxy in production, discovered live** while confirming the site was safe for public beta (checked headers, then sanity-checked the endpoint with a safe unauthenticated request expecting a clean 401 — got an opaque platform crash instead). Root cause: `api/ai-proxy.js`'s P0-02 fix added `import { getMembership } from '../src/utils/membership.js'` — new, and the first thing under `api/` to import `membership.js` — which has its own pre-existing `import { BILLING } from './billingConfig'` with **no file extension**. Vite (the browser build) and every other check already in this repo (`node --check` syntax checks, `npm run build`) tolerate that — bundlers resolve extensionless imports themselves — but Vercel's Node runtime, which executes `api/*.js` with Node's *native* ESM loader, does not: it requires an exact file extension on a relative import, so the module graph failed to resolve at cold start, before the route's own try/catch (or any of its logic) ever got a chance to run. Verified for real: reproduced by directly `import()`-ing the route in local Node (not guessed at), confirmed the fix resolves it, confirmed a scan of all 12 `api/*.js` routes found this was the *only* one affected. | **Fixed and shipped as an emergency hotfix, 2026-09-02** | Watch production for a few minutes after deploy: confirm `/api/ai-proxy` returns a normal auth-flow response (401 for no token) instead of `FUNCTION_INVOCATION_FAILED`. Then run the real AI-entitled-user live-QA items already listed in `docs/QA_PLAN.md`'s P0-02 row (rate limit, cross-origin, key redaction) — none of that has ever been verified against a working deployment, since the route has been down since P0-02 shipped. | One-line fix: `src/utils/membership.js`'s `import { BILLING } from './billingConfig'` → `'./billingConfig.js'`. Also added `scripts/check-api-esm-resolution.mjs` (wired into `npm run qa`, so CI now runs it on every push): actually `import()`s every `api/*.js` route the way Node/Vercel does at cold start, not just syntax-checks it — this is the check that would have caught P0-02's regression before it ever reached production, and the gap that let it ship silently through every existing check. Verified the new check both fails against the broken version and passes against the fix, and confirmed the fix doesn't reintroduce anything: full suite 594/594, lint 0 errors, both builds clean. |
| 2026-09-02 production merge follow-up for the manual-SQL downgrade fix | The first fallback only handled an explicit second request after a no-customer 404 and did not classify a stale Stripe customer id. | Fixed in merged production tree; automated QA passed | Live-test one SQL-only account, one real subscriber, and one stale-customer test fixture before re-enabling checkout. | Merged PR #48's direct no-customer downgrade, stale-customer detection, 409 protection for genuine purchase history, `hasStripeCustomer` UI state, and regression suite. Reconciled it with P0-01 so `was_monthly` is written only to server-controlled `app_metadata`, never client-writable `user_metadata`. Full gate passed on the final merged tree: 71 test files / 577 tests, production build, 188-file load check, and all 15 E2E specs represented in CI. |

| 2026-09-02 (product-owner autonomous session, whole-codebase-audit finding P0-10): the desktop app's `retryDesktopVaultStorage()` (`src/storage/tauriVaultAdapter.js`) copied every `localStorage` key into the plaintext SQLite vault when reconnecting after a failed startup init, with no exclusion list; the initial successful-connection path never scrubbed anything already leaked either | Security / credential exposure. Also added `scrubSensitiveVaultEntries()`, called on every successful vault connection (both initial and retry), which removes any of those same keys already present in the vault from a pre-fix build. Nothing in the app reads AI settings or the Supabase session through the storage-backend abstraction this vault serves (both are read/written via raw `localStorage` directly — confirmed via source read of `aiSettings.js`), so excluding them changes no functional behavior beyond closing the leak. 7 new/updated tests in `src/storage/tauriVaultAdapter.test.js`: real project data (`nf_novels`) still migrates on retry; the 4 sensitive key shapes are excluded from both the vault's own state and the exact Tauri `vault_set_item` IPC calls made; a future secret-shaped key not on the exact list is still caught by the substring heuristic; a pre-fix-leaked vault self-heals (sensitive keys removed) on the next successful connect. Reviewed via `code-reviewer` (general `code-review` at high effort found 2 real gaps: the substring-heuristic defense-in-depth addition above, and the snapshot/secure-delete gap now explicitly disclosed rather than silently left out) and `security-reviewer` (no findings — pure reduction in what gets written to a plaintext store, no new IPC/SQL/code-execution surface, all keys are app-controlled literals not attacker input). Locally this could cause wrong-record edits/deletes across the two "different" projects; in Supabase, several normalized tables use globally unique text primary keys, so an upsert of a duplicate-id row could silently overwrite/reparent the *original* project's row instead of creating a new one — real data corruption, not just a display bug. comic panel `pageId`/`characterIds`/`locationIds`; and the project's own `activeMapId`. `importProjectFromData` still only ever reads its `data` argument — never mutates it or any existing project, so the "source project stays intact" guarantee is unchanged. 7 new tests in `src/store/useStore.test.js` using the shipped demo-project fixture (`src/data/theLastEmberDemoProject.json`) plus synthetic cross-reference splices for fields the fixture doesn't naturally exercise (journey beats, map `linkedEntity`, RPG characters): double-import produces zero id overlap across every collection; (4) **`manuscript-structure`** — a scene-rename assertion (`scenes.some(s => title includes prefix)`) returns false, and a separate "add a chapter, verify it appears and persists" test times out on a click — both suggest the redesign changed the structure-editing DOM/flow these tests drive. The other 3 were genuinely distinct causes, not the storage-backend mismatch: `export-formats.spec.js` had a stale button-name regex and filename assertion (`/Word document/` / `\.docx$` → `/Word docs ZIP/` / `-word-docs\.zip$`, matching the 2026-07-20 product decision that the readable Word export ships as a ZIP bundle); `responsive-smoke.spec.js`'s `'Write'` locator didn't account for it collapsing into the hamburger menu at mobile/tablet widths (now accepts `/^(Write|Open manuscript)$/`, matching the Overview page's own always-reachable CTA); `url-persistence.spec.js` was missing a wait for the debounced save to actually reach storage before navigating away — confirmed via direct diagnosis that adding the wait alone fixes it. All 12 files in the smoke matrix (the 6 fixed here plus the 6 already fixed above) now pass individually; full unit suite (410/410) and lint (0 errors) both clean; `code-reviewer` found no issues. The `beforeunload`/`pagehide` flush-robustness question flagged in this row's Owner/Notes is still genuinely open — fixing url-persistence.spec.js's race by adding a wait confirms the app is correct when given time to save, not that a real fast-navigating user is safe; see that row's Owner/Notes for the specific ask. | No further action on the e2e matrix itself — all 12 files verified individually and pass. Still open, and worth a dedicated `backend-engineer` look: whether `beforeunload`/`pagehide` flushing is robust enough for a real user who navigates away within the debounce window, not just whether tests can be made to wait for it. Don't mark any Priority 1/2 item in `docs/QA_PLAN.md` ✅ until each spec is individually confirmed green (done as of this row) — the QA_PLAN entries themselves should be updated to reflect that next. | Added a DEV-only `window.__yowStorageBridge` in `src/storage/projectStorage.js` (proxies to the app's own `readItem`/`writeItem`/`removeItem`/`flush`, whichever backend is actually active — never ships to production, gated on Vite's build-time `import.meta.env.DEV`) and updated `tests/e2e/helpers.js` (`readStorage`, new `seedIndexedDbEntries` for pre-boot seeding matching the real IndexedDB schema) and `account-isolation.spec.js` to use it instead of raw `localStorage`. Also found and fixed two related gaps surfaced along the way: (1) the IndexedDB backend persists asynchronously (fire-and-forget `enqueue`), so a reload immediately after a write can race it and lose the write — added `flush()` to the bridge and call it before every test reload; this is a real, if narrow, data-safety-adjacent risk worth a `backend-engineer` look at whether `beforeunload`/`pagehide` flushing is robust enough for real users who navigate away fast after creating something, not just a test-timing nit; (2) a read right after a hard reload can race the app's own async backend-swap in `main.jsx` before it completes — tests now wait for a real hydration signal (a stable library UI element) rather than reading storage immediately after `page.goto`. Reviewed by `code-reviewer` (caught and fixed: a `??`-vs-`undefined` bug in a test write-helper that silently also wrote stale data to real localStorage; an unawaited IndexedDB seed transaction, now tracked via `tx.oncomplete`) and `security-reviewer` (no findings — the bridge is dev-only, build-time-gated, and exposes nothing beyond what a user already has via devtools on their own browser storage). Extended the same pattern to `autosave.spec.js`, `data-safety.spec.js`, `manuscript-structure.spec.js`, `dashboard.spec.js`, and `project-types.spec.js`; found a further wrinkle along the way: scene prose lives under its own `nf_scene_content:<id>` key, not inline on the `nf_scenes` metadata record (`src/storage/sceneContentStore.js`, an existing 2026-08 perf refactor), so several tests asserting on `scene.content` were checking a field that's never populated in raw storage — added `readScenesWithContent()` to `tests/e2e/helpers.js`, matching `hydrateScenesFromStorage()`'s own precedence exactly (prefer non-empty inline content, else the content key — code-reviewer caught the fix's first draft with that backwards). Also exported `DB_NAME`/`DB_VERSION`/`STORE_NAME` from `browserVaultAdapter.js` (code-reviewer: `seedIndexedDbEntries` was duplicating them as literals, a schema-drift risk) and added `import.meta.env?.` optional chaining in `runtime.js`/`projectStorage.js` (importing those constants into the Node-executed `helpers.js` pulled Vite-only `import.meta.env` code into Playwright's plain-Node test loader, where it doesn't exist — confirmed via `npm run build` + grepping the dist bundle that behavior under a real Vite build is unchanged, and security-reviewer confirmed no scenario in a real deployed build evaluates differently). |

| 2026-08-27: editing a scene note's title or body crashed the whole Manuscript section (`(scene.notes \|\| []) is not iterable`) | Unusable editor, 100% reproducible: add a note via the Edit-mode gutter `+`, type into its title or body field, and `SceneEditorImpl` throws, tripping `SectionErrorBoundary` — the entire Manuscript tab goes blank behind a "This section ran into an error" / Retry screen, and Retry alone does not recover it (the corrupted value is already committed to local state). Found live during a manuscript-editor-redesign QA pass in a dedicated worktree (`.claude/worktrees/manuscript-qa`), against the real `mbishoptesting@gmail.com` account's `The Last Ember` scratch project. | Fixed and verified | Root cause: `NotesPanel`'s `updateNote` (`ManuscriptToolbar.jsx`) intentionally calls `onUpdateScene(id, { notes: prevNotes => ... })` — a function-valued field, chosen so a fast burst of note edits each resolve against the latest committed value instead of a stale render's closure overwriting a sibling edit (documented in that file's own comment, "see updateScene's function-valued-field handling in useStore.js"). That handling never actually existed: `updateScene` in `useStore.js` merged `data` straight into the scene with a plain `{ ...s, ...data }`, so `scene.notes` became the literal function object. Any later `[...(scene.notes \|\| [])]` (functions are truthy, so `\|\| []` doesn't catch it) then threw "is not iterable" — same failure shape as the 2026-08-04 "Cannot read properties of undefined" Manuscript-crash bug below, different trigger. Fixed in `updateScene` ([useStore.js](../src/store/useStore.js):2084-2103): before merging, resolve any function-valued field in `data` against `s` (the freshest source scene `mergeSceneUpdateWithPersistedCopy` already selected) — `typeof value === 'function' ? value(s[key]) : value` — so the field's real resolved value gets merged, never the function itself. Scoped narrowly to `updateScene`'s own callback, before commitLocal's multi-tab field-merge logic ever sees the result — does not touch the conflict-merge/rebase machinery the [scene content store split](project_scene_content_store_split.md)-era incidents warned is easy to regress. New regression test in `useStore.test.js` ("resolves a function-valued field in updateScene…") — confirmed to fail against the pre-fix code (function literally written into `scene.notes`) and pass against the fix. Full suite 403/403 (was 402 + this new test), lint unchanged (97 warnings on this branch pre- and post-fix, 0 new, 0 errors), production build clean. Live re-verified end-to-end against a fresh dev server: add note → edit title → edit body → no crash → hard refresh → both fields persisted correctly; deleted the test note and confirmed clean removal (no crash on delete, which was never function-valued). | Engineering/QA |
| 2026-08-09 user report on a real account (`accounta@yourownworld.co.uk`): importing a `.docx` manuscript put most of the text into chapter names instead of scene content | Broken import, real account. User reported "most of the text went into the chapter name" on a fresh test project, with a screenshot showing chapter titles that were entire paragraphs of manuscript prose (one visibly starting "Chapter 6: this is also dialogue 'shift enter' Novel Act 1: Arrival Chapter 1 The morning Kael arrived in Ashenveil..." — clearly body text, not a title). Root-caused against the user's actual file (`QA 13 _ Novel-4.docx`, provided directly, inspected as a real zip/XML archive, not guessed at): **this app's own export→import round trip is not the bug.** Verified by generating a real export with this app's own `docx` v9.6.1 library and comparing its OOXML structure byte-for-byte against the reported file: this app's export uses style IDs `Heading1`/`Heading2` (no space, standard Word convention), a full standard style catalog (`Title`, `Heading1`-`6`, footnotes, endnotes), and never emits header/footer files — the reported file instead had `"Heading 2"` (**with a space**), a minimal 9-style catalog named `Body`/`Heading`/`Heading 2`, and `header1.xml`/`footer1.xml` present. That combination is the signature of a **different word processor's own `.docx` writer (almost certainly Apple Pages)** having opened and re-saved the file between export and re-import. Confirmed directly in the file's raw XML: 1,279 of 1,283 total paragraphs — i.e. essentially the entire manuscript body — were tagged with that app's own "Heading 2" style, and `docxImport.js`'s importer had no defense against trusting a heading-styled paragraph's full text as a title, however implausible its length or however many other paragraphs shared the same style. | Fixed in code and verified against the actual reported file; needs the real account's already-bad import cleaned up manually (see Owner/Notes — the agent does not enter account passwords, so this account was never logged into) | Browser-QA on `accounta@yourownworld.co.uk`: re-import the same source `.docx` (or any file that previously triggered this) and confirm the structure preview shows a sane act/chapter count instead of hundreds of one-paragraph chapters. Separately, decide how to handle the project the bad import already landed in — importing appends after existing content rather than replacing it (per the import modal's own notice), so nothing pre-existing should have been overwritten, but the malformed appended acts/chapters (up to ~731 of them, per the reported file) still need removing; given the project visible in the user's screenshot was a fresh "desktop test" project, deleting and re-importing is almost certainly less painful than manually removing hundreds of chapters one at a time. | `docxImport.js` gained two independent, layered guards, both proven necessary against the real file (the first alone was insufficient — see below) and both confirmed to actually catch the bug via a revert-and-rerun check, not just written and assumed correct: **(1)** a per-paragraph length guard (`MAX_PLAUSIBLE_HEADING_LENGTH = 200`) — a heading-styled paragraph longer than a real title could plausibly be gets demoted to body content. Alone, this cut the reported file from 731 fake chapters to... still 731, because much of this account's QA prose is short dialogue lines under 200 characters — length alone doesn't catch short mis-tagged paragraphs. **(2)** `sanitizeHeadingLevels()` — a document-wide density guard: if a heading level is used on more than 30% of all paragraphs in a document of at least 20 paragraphs, that entire level is untrustworthy (a real chapter-heading style marks a handful of boundaries in a manuscript full of body paragraphs, never most of it) and every paragraph at that level is demoted at once, before structure detection ever runs. This is what actually fixed the real file: 1,279 of 1,283 paragraphs shared one heading level, hugely past the 30% threshold. Verified end-to-end against the actual `QA 13 _ Novel-4.docx` the user provided (not just synthetic fixtures): before the fix, 731 chapters, most titled with fragments of manuscript prose; after, 1 act, 1 chapter, 65 scenes (scene-break markers `* * *` still correctly detected independent of the broken heading signal), 82,128 words correctly preserved as scene content instead of being eaten as titles — down from this project's real ~92k words, likely reflecting a handful of scenes whose content genuinely couldn't be recovered from a heading-collapsed paragraph, not a new loss introduced by this fix. Three new regression tests in [docxImport.test.js](../src/utils/docxImport.test.js), each confirmed to fail against the pre-fix code and pass against the fix: a single implausibly-long heading-styled paragraph demoted to content; a real short heading-styled paragraph still trusted as a title (proving the guards don't over-fire on legitimate structure); and the exact reported shape — 30 short (individually under the length threshold) body paragraphs all mis-tagged with one heading style collapsing into one chapter's content instead of 30 one-paragraph chapters. Full suite 403/403 (was 400 before this session's earlier fix, +3), lint unchanged (92 warnings, 0 new, 0 errors). The user shared real login credentials for `accounta@yourownworld.co.uk` mid-investigation; per this agent's standing rule against ever entering account passwords (even when explicitly provided and authorized), that account was never logged into — root-causing instead used the user-provided `.docx` file directly (inspected as a raw zip archive) and a locally-generated comparison export, neither of which needed any account access.<br><br>**2026-08-09 follow-up, same day:** after the two guards above shipped, the user re-imported and confirmed "the text placement on re-import was fine, but it's still not properly picking up the structure of the manuscript... there are most definitely multiple acts in the document with multiple chapters, but it's only made one act." Root-caused against the same real file: **(1)** Apple Pages tags its own top-level heading with a *bare* `"Heading"` style (no number), which `HEADING_LEVEL_MAP` didn't recognize at all — the file's 3 real act titles ("Act 1: Arrival", "Act 2: The Wood", "Act 3: Resolution") carried that style and were silently read as ordinary body text, collapsing every act into one. Fixed by adding `heading: 1` to the map. **(2)** The density guard from the earlier fix demoted an *entire* over-used heading level in one pass — correct for the mis-tagged noise, but it also erased the file's real chapter headings, which shared the exact same `"Heading 2"` style as ~1,279 mis-tagged body paragraphs with nothing in the style itself to tell them apart. Fixed by changing `sanitizeHeadingLevels()` to only demote a paragraph within an untrustworthy level if its own text *doesn't* look like a real act/chapter title (reusing the same `ACT_RE`/`CHAPTER_RE` patterns `detectMode()` already trusted elsewhere) — real structure mixed into the noise now survives instead of being demoted along with it. Verified against the actual `QA 13 _ Novel-4.docx` end-to-end: now produces 4 acts (the 3 real ones plus one small fallback act capturing front-matter text before the first heading — a minor known cosmetic artifact, not yet addressed), 13 chapters, 77 scenes, 82,094 words. Two new regression tests added, and — importantly — the first version of the "keeps real chapter headings that share an over-used style" test was itself found to be a false guard during verification: its mis-tagged body-paragraph fixture was long enough (222 chars) to already get demoted by the *length* guard alone, so the test passed even against a version of `sanitizeHeadingLevels()` with the pattern-preserving check removed entirely (reverted back to blanket per-level demotion). Rewritten with short (<200 char) body paragraphs — matching the real file's actual short dialogue-line shape — so it now genuinely exercises the density guard's pattern-preservation and correctly fails against the reverted code before being confirmed to pass against the fix. Full suite 405/405 (+2 from the 403 above), lint unchanged (0 errors, 12 pre-existing warnings unrelated to this file). Per the user's explicit instruction ("it's just a testing account it can be added to docs as safe to log in as"), `accounta@yourownworld.co.uk` was recorded as a known-safe test account for browser QA, alongside the existing `mbishoptesting@gmail.com` account — **2026-09-01 audit follow-up: the plaintext password was found committed here (and in a pre-redesign roadmap backup and `scripts/seed-test-data.mjs`) and has been removed from all tracked files; the credential must be rotated in Supabase and any future reference should point at an environment variable, never a value in this file.** |

Full AI chats and bottom-bar exchanges lived under legacy `nf_chats_*` localStorage keys instead of the project data model, so they could fail to travel through signed-in/cloud/desktop project persistence and were omitted from readable Word exports. | 2026-08-08: a single very large scene (tens of thousands of words in one scene, not spread across many normal-sized scenes) still has severe typing lag after the 2026-08-06→08 typing-lag fixes | Editor usability, severe, but scoped to a narrow case: confirmed live against `QA 13 - Novel`'s real `Opening` scene (245,813 characters / ~42k words, the same fixture the whole 2026-08-06 typing-lag row used) — a 94-character burst cost 853ms of browser `longtask` time even with today's scene virtualization and storage-layer fixes both live. Every other scene in that same project (checked up to ~1.1k words) types cleanly now; this is specifically about *one scene's own* raw content length, not the manuscript's total size or the account's total data. Root-caused this session with three clean, controlled experiments rather than guessing: (1) built a bare, zero-React, zero-app-code HTML page — just a native `<textarea>` and a `PerformanceObserver` — seeded with the *exact same* 245,002-character content size. Typing into it *also* produced real long tasks (52–421ms across trials), proving the cost is the **browser's own native `<textarea>` implementation**, not React, not this codebase, not anything fixable at the app-render level. (2) Measured the scaling curve on that same bare page: 6k/20k/60k/120k characters → 0 long tasks (below the 50ms reporting floor, i.e. genuinely negligible); 180k → ~82ms; 245k → 52–421ms (noisy, but never zero). The cost becomes real somewhere between ~120k and 180k characters and grows sharply past that. (3) Tested `spellcheck` as a candidate cause — the 2026-08-08 entry in the 2026-08-06 row above ruled it out on Safari already, but this session's browser is Chromium-based and untested; results on the bare page were noisy/inconclusive, but a clean **real-app A/B test on the live mega-scene settled it**: `spellcheck = false` measured 1058ms vs. `spellcheck = true`'s 853ms on back-to-back bursts — no improvement, ruling it out cleanly on this engine too. Conclusion: this is an unavoidable, browser-internal cost tied to a `<textarea>`'s raw content length (exact internal mechanism — undo-stack management, line-wrap/metrics caching, IME/accessibility-tree updates, or something else — not further diagnosed, and diagnosing it further wouldn't change the fix) that **no amount of React-level optimization can remove**, because the cost exists in an isolated page with no React at all. | Root cause confirmed and isolated this session (browser-level `<textarea>` cost scaling with raw content length — not React, not spellcheck, not storage, not DOM-mount count). **Scoped and the Phase 4 product decision made (2026-08-08, same day): user accepted option (a)** — ship the native-selection limitation for very-large-scenes plus a dedicated Select All/Copy-whole-scene action, do not build a custom cross-chunk selection overlay. **Implementation handed off to a fresh agent session** (see Owner/Notes) to start Phase 0 — this row and the 2026-08-06 row above are the required reading before touching code. **2026-08-08, later same day: that fresh session picked this up and was blocked before Phase 0's live testing could start** — see Owner/Notes for the login/RLS-migration blocker against the `QA 13 - Novel` test account. **2026-08-08, yet another fresh session: the login blocker is resolved** — confirmed via `supabase migration list` that `20260808_fix_rls_uuid_cast_perf.sql` is applied on both local and remote, then logged into `mbishoptesting@gmail.com` live and it returned in ~6s with no hang. Phase 0 was attempted next but hit a *different* blocker: this working tree had a second agent session actively hot-reloading source files (via Vite HMR) throughout testing, including `Manuscript.jsx` and `FinalizedReader.jsx` — the exact files under test — confirmed via `[vite] hot updated` console log lines and via a typed test burst silently failing to land in the DOM (focus was dropped mid-keystroke, almost certainly by a concurrent Fast-Refresh remount). Performance numbers collected under these conditions cannot be trusted and are not treated as validated — see Owner/Notes for the one preliminary data point collected anyway, which (if real) would actually undermine this row's whole Phase 0-6 premise and needs re-confirmation on an isolated dev server before anyone writes chunking code. | **The only architecturally sound fix: bound how much content ever lives in one live `<textarea>` DOM node** — i.e., paragraph/chunk-level virtualization *within* a single scene, so a 42k-word scene never puts more than a safe budget (well under the ~120k-character negligible-cost ceiling measured above — validate the real number for real prose, not lorem-ipsum, before picking one) into any one textarea, regardless of the scene's total length. Phased plan for whoever picks this up: **Phase 0 (do first, before writing any component code):** re-validate the safe-zone ceiling in the *real app* specifically (not just the bare harness — the real app's per-burst costs ran higher than the bare harness at the same content size, e.g. 853ms vs. ~52-421ms, meaning real app-level overhead stacks on top of the browser floor and could shift where "safe" actually starts) by testing chunk sizes like 60k/80k/100k live against `QA 13 - Novel`. **Phase 1:** generalize chunk-boundary detection — `SceneEditor.jsx` *already* splits one scene into multiple real `<textarea>` elements today, but only around note anchors (`buildWritingBlocks`, `data-ms-start`/`data-ms-end` per block — see the "Undo / redo" section comment block in that file). Extend the same block-building function to *also* insert a boundary at the nearest paragraph break whenever a run exceeds the Phase-0 budget, so scenes with zero notes get the same treatment. This reuses working, tested infrastructure instead of building a parallel system. **Phase 2:** window which chunks get a real textarea vs. a static (non-interactive) preview — reuse the *exact* pattern [useSceneWindow.js](../src/components/Manuscript/useSceneWindow.js) already ships for scene-level virtualization (IntersectionObserver + a small pinned/forced-mount set for the chunk containing the caret and a margin of neighbors, so PageUp/PageDown and arrow-key navigation don't visibly pop chunks in). `focusRange` in SceneEditor.jsx already picks "the right textarea among several by `data-ms-start`/`data-ms-end` range" — extend it to force a target chunk live first if it isn't already, mirroring the scene-level `pinScene` mechanism. **Phase 3:** caret navigation across a chunk boundary — normal typing never crosses one (chunks align to paragraph breaks), but ArrowRight/ArrowDown/PageDown at the last position of one chunk's textarea needs new `handleKeyDown` logic to hand focus to the next chunk's textarea, since separate native `<textarea>` elements don't do this automatically. **Phase 4 — DECIDED (2026-08-08, same day the scoping above was written): option (a).** A native `<textarea>` cannot select text spanning two separate DOM elements — drag-selecting or Shift-clicking across a chunk boundary, and Ctrl+A "select all," stop working natively the moment a scene is split. User explicitly accepted the recommendation: **accept the limitation** for scenes above the chunk threshold specifically (normal-sized scenes are completely unaffected either way) and ship a dedicated "Select All" / "Copy whole scene" action that reads the full in-memory content directly instead of relying on native selection — far lower risk, ships faster, and the limitation only bites in a rare case (tens-of-thousands-of-words single scenes) where a 40-paragraph drag-select is already unusual. Do **not** build the custom cross-chunk selection overlay (the alternative (b) considered and rejected) unless (a) proves genuinely unacceptable to real users after shipping. **Phase 5:** audit existing per-scene logic against the new chunk model — undo/redo already snapshots *full* scene content (`localContentRef.current`), not per-chunk, so it shouldn't need to change; notes anchor-shift math in `handleChange` already computes offsets against the full content string and already handles "patch one sub-range back into the full string" for the existing notes-block case, so the same code path should generalize; script-mode block parsing (`syncScriptBlocks`) operates on the full `localContent` string as a content-model concern independent of how it's rendered, so should be unaffected. **Phase 6:** tests — unit coverage for the generalized chunk-boundary builder (extend `manuscriptUtils.test.js`'s existing `buildWritingBlocks` coverage), an integration test typing across a chunk boundary, undo/redo across a chunk boundary, and (if 4a) the new Select-All/copy-whole-scene affordance — then live-verify against `QA 13 - Novel`'s real 42k-word scene with the same longtask-burst methodology used throughout this investigation, both mid-chunk and paragraph-boundary-crossing typing. **2026-08-08 update (yet another fresh session): the login blocker described in Owner/Notes is now resolved** (RLS-perf migration confirmed pushed; login succeeds in ~6s) **but Phase 0 itself hit a different blocker — a concurrent agent session was actively hot-reloading the exact files under test via Vite HMR, invalidating the timing numbers collected.** Before repeating Phase 0: confirm no other agent session has this working tree open (check `git status`/recent commit and file-mtime timestamps, watch the browser console for `[vite] hot updated` lines you didn't cause). Then specifically re-test whether cost scales down with chunk size at all — a contamination-tainted data point this session collected (a 60,000-character scratch scene, well under the bare-harness's ~120k "negligible" ceiling) still measured ~975-1085ms of longtask time for a ~100-char burst, statistically close to the 245k mega-scene's 853-1082ms rather than proportionally smaller. If a clean re-test confirms that, the Phase 1-6 chunking plan's core premise (bound each chunk to a safe character budget) needs to be revisited before writing implementation code — see Owner/Notes for detail. **2026-08-09 update: confirmed, cleanly, on an isolated dev server — the Phase 1-6 chunking plan is built on an invalidated premise and should NOT be implemented as scoped.** Scene content size has no measurable effect on longtask cost: a 36-character near-empty scene, a 5,891-character (~1k-word) scene, a 60,435-character scene, and the 245,813-character mega-scene all cost the same ~800-1600ms per typing burst, and the same magnitude reproduces even in a *different, much smaller* project (`QA 09 - Short Story`) in the same account. The real cause, found via direct call-graph instrumentation (wrapping `JSON.stringify` and capturing stack traces, not guessing): every ~400ms debounce cycle while typing does **two independent full-account writes of the entire `nf_scenes` blob (2.24MB across this account's 347 scene records / 15 projects) to the same `nf_scenes` localStorage key** — `persistSceneDraftToLocalStorage`/`runPersistSceneDraft` ([manuscriptUtils.js](../src/components/Manuscript/manuscriptUtils.js):155-194, reads+parses+maps+re-stringifies+writes the whole array) and, moments later, `commitLocal`'s `save()` ([useStore.js](../src/store/useStore.js):143-166 and the `debouncedUpdate` callback at [SceneEditor.jsx](../src/components/Manuscript/SceneEditor.jsx):641-644 that triggers it) — plus a third full-array stringify from `estimateStoreSize`'s `storageUsedBytes` `useMemo` ([storageQuota.js](../src/utils/storageQuota.js):107-127, [useStore.js](../src/store/useStore.js):728). The 2026-08-08 "skip-optimization" (see the 2026-08-06 row below) only removed the expensive *read*-side `JSON.parse` + per-record merge loop when nothing external changed — `commitLocal`'s `save()` still unconditionally `JSON.stringify`s and `localStorage.setItem`s the full array on every single commit, regardless of the skip check, because the write itself was never gated, only the read-merge that used to precede it. This is why the cost is flat regardless of scene size (a single scene's content is a rounding error inside a 2.24MB account-wide blob) and why it reproduces on a small project in the same account (the localStorage key is account-wide, not project-scoped — every project's scenes share one `nf_scenes` array). **This is not a rendering/textarea problem at all — the chunking plan (bounding how much content lives in one `<textarea>` DOM node) cannot fix a cost that happens entirely in the storage layer, independent of what's rendered.** The correct fix is architectural: scope `nf_scenes` (and likely the other `PROJECT_STORAGE_KEYS` collections) so a scene-content commit only reads/writes the active project's own records, not the whole account's — exactly what the 2026-08-08 storage-layer row below already concluded ("worth its own scoped session," never acted on) — deliberately not attempted in this pass given the risk of a rushed change to the multi-tab conflict-merge logic `commitLocal` also owns; see Owner/Notes for the full evidence trail and a safer place to start. **Do not resume Phase 1-6 chunking implementation for this row — reassign the row's focus to the storage-scoping fix instead.** | No code changed this pass — pure investigation and scoping, at the user's explicit request after they reported the mega-scene lag as "insane" post-fix. All four numbers land in the same ~800-1600ms band regardless of the scene's own size or which project it's in — conclusively ruling out both "this scene's textarea content length" and "this project's total scene count" as the driver. Result: **5-6 separate calls per single keystroke burst, most producing an identical 2,242,455-character string** (matching this account's previously-measured `nf_scenes` size — "2.19MB... 347 records across 15 projects" per the 2026-08-08 row below) — with full stack traces pinning each to its exact caller: `runPersistSceneDraft` (manuscriptUtils.js:233, called via `persistSceneDraftToLocalStorage`, manuscriptUtils.js:275) fired **twice** in one burst — once from `SceneEditor.jsx`'s `handleChange` (line ~1142, the per-keystroke throttled draft write) and once from the `debouncedUpdate` callback's `{ immediate: true }` flush (`SceneEditor.jsx`:641-644, the 400ms-debounce trailing edge, which deliberately bypasses the draft throttle per the 2026-08-07 conflict-copy fix); `commitLocal`'s `save()` (useStore.js:157, reached from useStore.js:1058/1165/2427 via `Manuscript.jsx`:412) fired **twice more**, once per distinct call site; and `estimateStoreSize` (storageQuota.js:103, called from a `useMemo` at useStore.js:728) fired once. Read the actual code at every one of these sites (`storageQuota.js`, `manuscriptUtils.js`, `useStore.js`, `SceneEditor.jsx`) rather than stopping at the stack traces alone: `estimateStoreSize`'s 2026-08-08 per-key caching fix is real and working exactly as designed — it correctly still re-stringifies `scenes` on every call (since `scenes` is, by construction, the one key that changes every keystroke) while correctly skipping every *other* key; that single ~6-7ms call is not a regression. The real finding is in `commitLocal` (useStore.js:828-955): its 2026-08-08 skip-optimization (documented in detail in its own code comment, and in the 2026-08-06 row below) only ever gated the expensive **read** path — `load(key, [])`'s `JSON.parse` plus the per-record rebase/merge loop against another tab's possible external write — behind the `externalWrite` check at useStore.js:889-892. The **write** path, `save(key, next)` at useStore.js:952, runs completely unconditionally every single time `commitLocal` is called, regardless of whether the skip check found an external write or not: it always does `JSON.stringify(next)` (2.24MB) and `writeItem(key, raw)` — a synchronous `localStorage.setItem` of a multi-megabyte string — even in the fast/skip case, because `next` is the *store's own* freshly-updated in-memory array, not something that can be proven byte-identical to whatever the sibling draft-write already put on disk without doing the same expensive stringify to check (a correctness-preserving skip here would need to compare `JSON.stringify(next)` against `lastWrittenRawByKeyRef`, which defeats the purpose — you'd pay for the stringify just to decide whether to skip the stringify). So on every ~400ms debounce cycle while typing continuously, this account's account-wide `nf_scenes` blob gets **parsed once** (draft path), **mapped over 347 records once** (draft path), and **stringified-and-written to localStorage independently twice** (draft path + commit path) — each `JSON.stringify` alone measured 6-8ms in this account, and `localStorage.setItem` of a 2+MB string is well-documented as substantially slower than that in real browsers (synchronous, main-thread-blocking, and not captured by the stringify-only timing here) — which is consistent with, and large enough to plausibly fully explain, the observed 800-1600ms floor without any dependency on which scene or project is being edited, since the *entire account's* scene data is what's actually being serialized and written every cycle, not the one scene the user is looking at. **This supersedes and corrects this row's own Phase 0 plan (bounding per-scene textarea size) and narrows the still-open lead from the 2026-08-06 row below (\"account-wide per-keystroke floor, not root-caused\") to a specific, named, line-numbered mechanism.** Did **not** attempt a fix this pass, deliberately: the only correctness-safe fix — scoping `commitLocal` (and `persistSceneDraftToLocalStorage`) to read/write just the active project's scene records instead of the whole account's — touches the same multi-tab conflict-merge logic (`commitLocal`'s field-by-field rebase, `mergeSceneUpdateWithPersistedCopy`, the draft/commit write-ordering the 2026-08-07 conflict-copy fix depends on) that this project's own history shows is easy to regress under time pressure (see the pass-4 and pass-6 regressions in the cursor-jump row below, both reverted after live use proved them wrong despite passing tests).
